      "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nWe adopt Differential Item Functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to assess whether individual benchmark items behave consistently across groups of LLMs. The procedure requires only a binary response matrix and group labels, with no access to model internals or training data. Below we define the data, the statistical test, the cohort designs, and a semi-synthetic validation framework.\n\n\\subsection{Data}\n\\label{sec:data}\n\nThe primary dataset is the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, which records binary outcomes (1~=~correct, 0~=~incorrect) for 5,227 models evaluated on 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}. Models span release dates from 2023 to 2024, drawn from the Open LLM Leaderboard. MMLU covers 57 subjects grouped into five broad domains (STEM, Humanities, Social Sciences, Other, and a mixed category). We supplement the response matrix with the web-overlap contamination labels of \\citet{li2024contamination}, which classify 9,229 of 12,508 items (73.8\\% coverage) as ``contaminated'' or ``clean'' based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible web corpora. These labels serve as an external reference for evaluating the relationship between DIF flags and existing contamination indicators.\n\n\\subsection{Mantel-Haenszel DIF and ETS Classification}\n\\label{sec:mhdif}\n\nIn educational testing, Differential Item Functioning (DIF) occurs when an item's statistical relationship to the latent trait $\\theta$ differs between two groups after conditioning on ability level. An item with DIF is not merely harder for one group; it functions differently relative to what the group's overall ability would predict. The standard terminology refers to test-takers as ``examinees'' and questions as ``items.'' In our setting, examinees are LLMs and items are benchmark questions.\n\nThe Mantel-Haenszel (MH) procedure~\\citep{holland1988mh,magis2010difr} detects DIF by stratifying examinees into $K$ score bins based on total score (a proxy for $\\theta$) and computing a common odds ratio across strata. We use $K = 5$ equiprobable strata (quintile-based binning) following ETS recommended practice for large-scale assessments~\\citep{donoghue1993mh,clauser1998dif}; $K{=}3$ yields near-identical power (\\Cref{app:k3_power}). Within each stratum $k$, the responses of the reference group and the focal group form a $2 \\times 2$ contingency table:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mh}\n\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = \\frac{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} A_k D_k \\,/\\, T_k}{\\displaystyle\\sum_{k=1}^{K} B_k C_k \\,/\\, T_k}\\,,\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\noindent where $A_k$ and $B_k$ are the number of correct and incorrect responses in the reference group for stratum $k$, $C_k$ and $D_k$ are the corresponding counts for the focal group, and $T_k$ is the total number of examinees in stratum $k$. Under the null hypothesis of no DIF, $\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}} = 1$. The log odds ratio is converted to the ETS delta scale:\n\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:delta}\n\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}} = -2.35 \\ln(\\alpha_{\\mathrm{MH}})\\,.\n\\end{equation}\n\nThe Educational Testing Service (ETS) classifies DIF severity into three categories based on $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}|$. Category~A (negligible) requires $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$. Category~C (large) requires both $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and statistical significance at $p < 0.05$ from the MH chi-squared test. Category~B (moderate) includes all remaining items: those with $1.0 \\leq |\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.5$, or $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ without statistical significance. The dual threshold in ETS~C (effect size \\emph{and} significance) guards against flagging items that show large but unreliable effect sizes. These thresholds were calibrated for human test-taker populations; we retain them as standardized reference points and verify robustness across stricter cutoffs (\\Cref{sec:temporal}). Throughout this paper, we report the percentage of items classified as ETS~C, denoted C\\%. At $N = 1{,}887$ (the temporal comparison; 5{,}227 total across all cohort designs), significance ($p < 0.05$) is nearly automatically satisfied for any $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$: only 3 of 3{,}918 C items lack significance, so the dual criterion effectively reduces to a single effect-size threshold; per-item DEFF correction (\\Cref{app:deff}) restores $p$-value selectivity by reducing effective $N$: under simple-mean DEFF, 269 items (6.9\\%) lose significance while retaining $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; 74\\% of these are near-miss ($p_{\\mathrm{adj}} \\in [0.05, 0.10]$).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_ets_distribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Distribution of $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across 12,508 MMLU items (2023 vs.\\ 2024 cohorts). Colors indicate ETS severity: A (negligible, $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| < 1.0$), B (moderate), C (large, $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$ and $p < 0.05$). The heavy tails show that 31.3\\% of items exhibit large DIF, 50$\\times$ above random baselines (\\Cref{sec:temporal}).}\n\\label{fig:ets_distribution}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe choice of matching variable (the total score used for stratification) affects both validity and interpretation. We distinguish three matching strategies: (1)~\\emph{standard matching} uses the within-benchmark total score on all items of the same benchmark; (2)~\\emph{cross-subject matching}, adapting purified subtest matching~\\citep{swaminathan1990logistic} to the LLM multi-subject setting, uses the total score on all subjects \\emph{other than} the one containing the item under test, preventing the matching variable from absorbing injected contamination signals (\\Cref{sec:injection}); and (3)~\\emph{external matching} uses the total score on a different benchmark entirely (e.g., MMLU total score as the matching variable when testing DIF on Grade School Math 8K (GSM8K) items), providing an independent ability proxy uncontaminated by the target benchmark. Cross-subject and external matching both address matching variable contamination but at different granularities: cross-subject operates within a multi-domain benchmark, while external operates across benchmarks. Total score matching is adequate: IRT $\\theta$ correlates at $r = 0.9986$ with total scores (\\Cref{sec:structural}).\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-specific challenges.} Applying MH-DIF to LLMs introduces four challenges absent in educational testing. First, overlapping training corpora violate local independence; permutation simulation (1{,}000 replications shuffling group labels within score strata) confirms local-dependence-attributable FPR inflation $< 0.001$~pp under cross-subject matching (\\Cref{app:method_details}). 1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
5	
6	\begin{figure*}[t]
7	\centering
8	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}
9	\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\ random item removal. DIF-ordered removal (orange) degrades $\rho$ faster than random (blue band = mean $\pm$ 1\,SD, 20 seeds). Dashed lines: Monitor/Flag (15\%) and Flag/Act (25\%) thresholds (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).}
10	\label{fig:rho_degradation}
11	\end{figure*}
12	
13	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
14	\label{sec:validation}
15	
16	\looseness=-1
17	A Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \geq 100$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 (under uniform injection) and area under the curve (AUC) of 0.93--0.94 for $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$ (\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).
18	
19	\subsection{Temporal DIF: ${\sim}$29\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}
20	\label{sec:temporal}
21	
22	\looseness=-1
23	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\% CI: $[29.2\%, 37.8\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$; \Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2\% and weighted-mean yields 13.5\%, bracketing C\% at 13.5--29.2\%; the cluster bootstrap CI excludes the weighted-mean estimate, providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound (\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation,app:icc_family}). Random permutations within the 2023 cohort confirm the ETS~C threshold maintains per-item FPR below 1\% (99th percentile $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| = 1.45$); no non-dominant family drives the signal (leave-one-family-out [LOFO] $|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp; \Cref{tab:lofo,app:dedup_k}).
24	
25	\looseness=-1
26	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\% of 0.62\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\% (2024; 100 permutations), 24--50$\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$; at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
27	
28	\looseness=-1
29	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). DIF is item-specific: domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance (\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($r = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The temporal DIF finding persists across thresholds ($18.4\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
30	
31	\begin{table}[t]
32	\centering
33	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. C\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\ labels are not applicable.}
34	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
35	\small
36	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
37	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
38	\toprule
39	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & C\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\
40	\midrule
41	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
42	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,090 / ${\sim}$2,090 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
43	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
44	Random split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\
45	Random split 2024 (sanity) & ${\sim}$400 / ${\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\
46	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
47	\bottomrule
48	\end{tabular}}
49	\end{table}
50	
51	\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}
52	\label{sec:groundtruth}
53	
54	\looseness=-2
55	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$; $p = 0.88$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Label-independent evidence further supports mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\chi^2 = 562.7$; \Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning}: complementary diagnostic dimensions (\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).
56	
57	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
58	\label{sec:structural}
59	
60	\looseness=-1
61	Two structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; cross-subject C\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\% (\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\% converges to ${\sim}$28--29\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\Cref{app:structural}).
62	
63	\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}
64	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
65	
66	\looseness=-2
67	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ (\Cref{fig:matching}). The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} \approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$, still insufficient to account for the observed 31\% C\% (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Difficulty-dependent injection yields $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\sim}$20\% (\Cref{app:method_details}).
68	
69	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
70	\label{sec:downstream}
71	
72	\looseness=-2
73	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, mean $\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\leq 2.9\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average (\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).
74	
75	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
76	\label{sec:gsm8k}
77	
78	\looseness=-2
79	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks, including ARC (AI2 Reasoning Challenge), consistently higher than procedural ones (C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ vs.\ $17.9$--$18.9\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.
80

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}
5	\label{sec:disc_temporal}
6	
7	\looseness=-2
8	Temporal DIF detects \emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \emph{why}, by design (\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\% focal-favoring ($\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, pointing to non-contamination drivers (\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\Delta = 1.08$\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not a cohort-structure artifact (\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}). Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: C\% (29.2\%) substantially exceeds typical rates~\citep{clauser1998dif}; DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.
9	
10	\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}
11	\label{sec:disc_protocol}
12	
13	\looseness=-2
14	Item-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\% exceeds $20\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\emph{Triage}: DIF $\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\Cref{app:score_inflation}).
15	
```

      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, pointing to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not a cohort-structure artifact (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}). Three features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: C\\% (29.2\\%) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); and the base/instruct reversal (OR $= 4.75$) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",
      "numLines": 15,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 15
    }
  },

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "d4db0e1b-608e-4a18-b16b-63cc47ed54ba",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "1a462dee-42e9-4b28-bf52-a3fecdeb02e8",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH",
    "newString": "with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we assume that every test item measures the same construct for both models. In educational testing, this property, \\emph{measurement invariance}~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, is routinely verified before scores are compared across groups; in LLM evaluation, it has never been tested at scale. We test it here, applying Mantel-Haenszel differential item functioning (DIF) analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with ETS severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items). The result is sobering: 31\\% of items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, compared to 0.62\\% under random-split controls, and the finding replicates across all six METABENCH benchmarks (17.9--31.3\\%).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 31.3\\% of items flagged as C (29.2\\% after DEFF correction), yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThis instability is not what it appears. Aggregate rankings are near-perfect ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$): even after all unstable items are removed, the leaderboard barely changes. But individual items have shifted in structured ways: some became easier for newer models independent of overall ability, others harder, and these differential effects can shift individual models by ${\\pm}561$ rank positions at the 99th percentile (\\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). Instability does not threaten validity; it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations. Base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), which confirms structured capability changes, not noise.\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThis diagnostic signal is distinct from what existing benchmark integrity methods target. Contamination detection asks whether items appear in training corpora, probing \\emph{global exposure}~\\citep{shi2024mink,dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Measurement invariance asks whether items \\emph{function equivalently} across model cohorts (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard~$= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones. Any item-level detector validated solely against web-overlap labels is blind to differential functioning.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_1_global_vs_differential.pdf}\n\\caption{Global vs.\\ differential contamination via Item Characteristic Curves (ICCs). \\textbf{Left}: global contamination produces no DIF; both cohorts encounter the item, and their ICCs overlap. \\textbf{Right}: differential contamination produces DIF; the focal cohort's ICC shifts due to disproportionate exposure. The enrichment ratio of $\\approx 1.0$ between DIF flags and web-overlap labels is expected because most items fall into the left category.}\n\\label{fig:global_vs_differential}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTo our knowledge, this is the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementary to concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 31.3\\% of MMLU items exhibit large temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks; 29.2\\% after non-independence correction), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), consistent with structured capability shifts rather than uniform exposure (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Semi-synthetic validation with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: cross-subject matching eliminates false-positive inflation under single-subject contamination ($k \\leq 5$), portable across benchmarks (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{DIF as diagnostic lens with actionable audit protocol}: unstable items carry disproportionate cross-cohort signal ($z = -8$ to $-49\\sigma$) yet are the least ranking-disruptive under non-random removal, which confirms that DIF isolates differential functioning rather than generally informative items; we propose a Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol for benchmark maintainers (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

  "content": "[飞书用户 曹晓玉]: 🔍 论文自检：所有引用的逐项正确性检查（交叉验证）\n\n请对论文里**每一条引用**做严格核对。AI 写作最致命的问题之一就是 hallucinate 引用——作者错、年份错、venue 错、标题对不上，甚至整篇论文压根不存在。\n\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n检查范围\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n对 .bib 里的每一条 entry，以及正文里每一个 \\cite{} 出现的位置：\n\n□ 作者名拼写（中间名 / 缩写格式 / Unicode 字符）\n□ 年份（很多 AI 把 NeurIPS 2022 paper 写成 2021）\n□ 标题（前几个词最容易被 hallucinate 改写）\n□ Venue / 会议名（NeurIPS vs NIPS / EMNLP vs EMNLP Findings / arXiv vs published version）\n□ 卷期号 / 页码（journal 引用）\n□ DOI / arXiv ID（如果有）\n□ Paper 是否真实存在（hallucinated paper 是最严重的硬伤）\n□ 正文里的 cite 用得对吗（cite 了 paper A 但说的是 paper B 的结论）\n\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n执行方式：Main Agent 派 2-3 个 Worker 做交叉验证\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\nMain Agent 调用 spawn_worker 起 2-3 个独立 Worker，每个 Worker 独立完成全部引用的核对：\n\n- 每个 Worker 拿到完整的 .bib 文件 + 正文 \\cite 位置列表\n- 用 Semantic Scholar API / Google Scholar / arXiv 搜索每一条\n- 独立产出一份\"引用核对清单\"：每条标 ✓ 或 ✗ + 错误描述\n\nMain Agent 收到所有 Worker 的清单后做交叉比对：\n\n- ✅ 所有 Worker 都标 OK 的 → 接受\n- ⚠️ Worker 之间结论不一致的 → Main Agent 亲自再核一遍\n- ❌ 多数 Worker 标错的 → 必须修\n\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n修复策略\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n- 作者 / 年份 / 标题错 → 改成正确的\n- venue 错（如 arxiv 版本和会议版本搞混）→ 改成应该引用的版本\n- Paper 不存在（hallucinated）→ 删 cite + 重新找一个真实的同主题 paper 替代，如果找不到替代且这个 claim 离开 cite 就站不住，整段重写或删\n- cite 用错位置（A 引到了 B）→ 改成正确的 cite key\n\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n汇报内容\n══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n(a) 总共多少条引用？\n(b) 每个 Worker 各标出多少处问题？Worker 之间一致率？\n(c) Main Agent 交叉比对后，最终确认有问题的引用数量\n(d) 每一处的处理方式（修了 / 删了 / 替换了哪条新引用）\n(e) 修完后 .bib 重新生成 + LaTeX 编译通过 + bibtex 没 warning"
}

### User

_source line 1189 · 2026-05-26T10:13:50.446Z_

[飞书用户 曹晓玉]: 🔍 论文自检：所有引用的逐项正确性检查（交叉验证）

请对论文里**每一条引用**做严格核对。AI 写作最致命的问题之一就是 hallucinate 引用——作者错、年份错、venue 错、标题对不上，甚至整篇论文压根不存在。

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
检查范围
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

对 .bib 里的每一条 entry，以及正文里每一个 \cite{} 出现的位置：

□ 作者名拼写（中间名 / 缩写格式 / Unicode 字符）
□ 年份（很多 AI 把 NeurIPS 2022 paper 写成 2021）
□ 标题（前几个词最容易被 hallucinate 改写）
□ Venue / 会议名（NeurIPS vs NIPS / EMNLP vs EMNLP Findings / arXiv vs published version）
□ 卷期号 / 页码（journal 引用）
□ DOI / arXiv ID（如果有）
□ Paper 是否真实存在（hallucinated paper 是最严重的硬伤）
□ 正文里的 cite 用得对吗（cite 了 paper A 但说的是 paper B 的结论）

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
执行方式：Main Agent 派 2-3 个 Worker 做交叉验证
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

Main Agent 调用 spawn_worker 起 2-3 个独立 Worker，每个 Worker 独立完成全部引用的核对：

- 每个 Worker 拿到完整的 .bib 文件 + 正文 \cite 位置列表
- 用 Semantic Scholar API / Google Scholar / arXiv 搜索每一条
- 独立产出一份"引用核对清单"：每条标 ✓ 或 ✗ + 错误描述

Main Agent 收到所有 Worker 的清单后做交叉比对：

- ✅ 所有 Worker 都标 OK 的 → 接受
- ⚠️ Worker 之间结论不一致的 → Main Agent 亲自再核一遍
- ❌ 多数 Worker 标错的 → 必须修

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
修复策略
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

- 作者 / 年份 / 标题错 → 改成正确的
- venue 错（如 arxiv 版本和会议版本搞混）→ 改成应该引用的版本
- Paper 不存在（hallucinated）→ 删 cite + 重新找一个真实的同主题 paper 替代，如果找不到替代且这个 claim 离开 cite 就站不住，整段重写或删
- cite 用错位置（A 引到了 B）→ 改成正确的 cite key

══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
汇报内容
══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════

(a) 总共多少条引用？
(b) 每个 Worker 各标出多少处问题？Worker 之间一致率？
(c) Main Agent 交叉比对后，最终确认有问题的引用数量
(d) 每一处的处理方式（修了 / 删了 / 替换了哪条新引用）
(e) 修完后 .bib 重新生成 + LaTeX 编译通过 + bibtex 没 warning
